Deep CNN chaotic key generator for multi-parameter elliptic curves over cybersecurity image encryption application
Authors: Aliaa M. Alabdali, Esam A. A. Hagras
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
A chaotic image encryption technique is introduced relying on efficient non-linear substitution boxes (NL-S boxes) based on Deep convolutional neural networks chaotic key generator and new multi-parameter-multi-prime elliptic curves (Deep-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC). Deep convolutional neural networks are used in this image encryption algorithm to generate an unexpected key stream. Image encryption system can benefit from the unexpected key stream sequence. In the presented method, a new cascaded 2D sine–cosine cross–chaotic map (cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map), and this chaotic map is used to generate the multi-parameter and the multi-prime for the elliptic curve’s generation. All the elliptic curves tack their parameters based on the pseudo-random sequences produced by the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map. Furthermore, the points produced by the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map and the generated elliptic curves are utilized to generate dynamic NL-S boxes and dynamic permutation tables. The updated NL-S boxes can be established by changing the shared elliptic curve key and the secure chaotic parameters. A reliable image encryption approach is designed based on the proposed Deep-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC and the generated strong S-boxes. Finally, security analysis and simulation results confirm the reliability of the suggested encryption scheme, and it can be used as a robust encryption scheme.

Introduction

Artificial intelligent (AI) in cybersecurity offers enterprises looking to strengthen their security frameworks both opportunities and problems. Because cybersecurity professionals are not well-versed in AI approaches and their benefits, this shift is not smooth. Nevertheless, integrating AI into cybersecurity initiatives frequently results in a number of desired advantages, which can only be attained by recognizing the uniqueness and difficulties of doing so. Deep learning has become a new trend in scientific research because it can efficiently represent complex functions using a multi-layer network structure. It is employed in many different applications, including image processing, image classification1,2, object picture segmentation3,4 and detection5,6. The Generative Adversarial Network (GAN), which is made up of a discriminator and a generator, is one of the subfields of deep learning7. While the discriminator must determine whether the input is authentic data or a fabricated sample, the generator must monitor the distribution of the sample data. The deep-learning model, which has a complicated network architecture, a random training procedure, and a huge number of parameters, is an alternative to the stream cypher generator.

A new approach for encrypting chaotic block images based on a deep convolutional generated adversarial network (SCGANs) was presented in8, and the Feistel network was enhanced. Moreover, a deep and hyperchaotic convolutional adversarial network in conjunction with a novel system has been suggested. The main objective was to provide a more intricate and unpredictable key stream of random numbers. In addition, the researchers presented a stream cypher generator and a deep learning-based streaming cypher generator that generates the private key needed to encrypt and decrypt medical images in9. Work has recently been done to introduce the deep convolutional neural network-based picture encryption approach and the chaotic algorithm-based key generation method10. A new secure SIELNet encryption method for color photos has just been established in industry 5.0 technology11–13. The authors initially used a new three-dimensional chaotic map to encrypt color images. This chaotic map has good chaotic behavior and generates cypher images that are similar to the source images. Second, the authors use a customized residual dense spatial network to rebuild a lossy image from a compressed, encrypted image in order to address the constrained super-resolution problem.

Deep neural networks are used in these picture encryption algorithms to generate an unexpected key stream. Image encryption systems can benefit from the unexpected key stream sequence. A deep-auto-encoder-based encryption technique was introduced by Sang et al.11 to create a highly random ciphertext image, which is essential for an encryption system. These strategies make use of deep neural networks to produce quantifiable results and hidden keys, which encourages research into more effective ways to combine deep learning with chaotic systems.

Chaotic systems show a high sensitivity to slight changes in both initial conditions and parameters. This means that teeny changes in the initial conditions or parameters of the map induce a considerable variation in its output. This high sensitivity characteristic results in the failure of prediction ability for chaotic systems in future states. A popular measure to quantify the chaotic dynamics of a dynamical system is the Lyapunov exponent (LE)14. A positive LE value/values indicates that a given dynamical system is chaotic. Plotting the chaotic map’s sequences in conjunction with parameter changes is known as the bifurcation diagram (BD). Numerous investigations of a chaotic map-based image encryption scheme are presented in15–21. One-dimensional chaotic maps have some disadvantages, including a few control parameters, an easy transition to periodic mapping, and a limited range of chaotic possibilities, despite their simplicity, low complexity, and ease of implementation22. However, because of their high computational costs and difficulty in hardware implementation, multi-dimensional chaotic maps with two or more state variables perform better and have more unpredictable chaotic orbits. A Nonlinear Cascade Chaotic System (NL-CCS) was designed to extend the range of control parameters23–25. In26, a new integrated chaotic system employed for image encryption was unveiled, based on the NL-CCS principle. The authors in27 proposed a cascaded technique for building new chaotic maps with enhanced unpredictability to enhance the randomization of the NL-CCS. A generalized approach to chaos cascade and its uses are presented in28. Recent reports in29,30 describe a new n-dimensional polynomial chaotic system that combines DNA coding with a 2D cascaded logistic map and a cascade chaotic map.

In many symmetric chaotic and cryptographic systems, an S-box is the only thing to blame for confusion and diffusion in the input data. The S-box is a key element of numerous well-known block ciphers. Some block ciphers have recently been presented that rely on the idea of dynamic S-boxes, in which plain text is encrypted using random S-boxes generated by a dynamic S-box generator31–33. In addition, a symmetric chaotic encryption scheme based on the S-Box for the diffusion property has recently been presented in34–38. A unique encryption method based on the permutation of a symmetric group and employing multiple semifield S-boxes is proposed in39. In40, a novel spatiotemporal chaotic system-based picture encryption approach using random blocks and dynamic S-boxes is given. In41, based on a chaotic Mandelbrot map, a set of robust S-box constructions was implemented and applied in image encryption. A technique for creating replacement boxes via random selection is introduced in42. The hyperchaotic system-based S-box generation algorithm and its use in image encryption are discussed in43. A social network search algorithm based on a roulette wheel is also used in44 to generate and optimize substitute boxes. Investigating a chaos-based algorithm for S-box construction and optimization utilizing Q-learning is proposed in45.

The idea of a discrete logarithmic problem on cryptosystems-based public key elliptic curves (PK-ECs) is put forth in46,47. PK-ECs are a popular option for secure communication due to their superior cryptographic properties. Strong S-boxes can be constructed using a variety of techniques found in the literature. A bijective S-box is defined as a suitable bijection that maps m input bits on m output bits. We concentrate on the elliptic curve theory-based construction of S Boxes. A novel image encryption method based on elliptic curves is presented in48. Specifically, using points on the elliptic curve, the authors introduce a novel method for building S-boxes over a prime field. The proposed approach generates the cryptographically strong S-box\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{b}^{a}$$\end{document} by using the x-coordinates of ordered pairs of a specified elliptic curve over prime field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{p\:}\left(a,b\right)$$\end{document}, where p is a prime bigger than 289 and a and b are chosen from the finite field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{p}$$\end{document}. When compared to similar cryptosystems, the comparison shows that the proposed method yields advantageous strong S-boxes. The authors of49 provided an efficient method for generating uncorrelated and cryptographically robust injective S-boxes using ordered isomorphic ECs. The experimental results show that the newly discovered method may successfully generate dynamic, uncorrelated, and cryptographically safe S-boxes. As a result, the recommended method for encrypting data that is closely related is secure.

An inventive and successful method for constructing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:8\times\:8$$\end{document} S-boxes is detailed in50. This strategy is limited to even numbers between 2 and 254 that do not contain multiples of 4. It makes use of a finite field and polynomial mapping. The newly constructed S-box exhibits better performance against intrusion attempts when compared to recent S-boxes in the literature. A genuinely dynamic replacement box generator for block ciphers employing elliptic curves over finite rings is proposed by the authors in51. The primary contribution of51 is a quick and secure public-key image encryption system with an efficient and flexible substitution-box generator. In52, a hybrid picture encryption method makes use of the ordered elliptic curve (OEC), pseudo random numbers (PRN), and dynamic S-box. S-boxes are constructed using the OEC’s x-coordinates following the application of modulo operations, whereas PRN is created on an OEC using a generalized Frobenius map. The suggested method is effective and demonstrates strong defense against contemporary attacks, according to a thorough analysis and comparisons with more recent picture encryption techniques. It is also recommended to use different chaotic maps over elliptic curves to build S-Boxes for picture encryption53. A very dynamic and efficient substitution-box generator based on finite elliptic curves is designed in54. A novel deterministic replacement box generator for real-time applications over elliptic curves is introduced in55. In56, robust S-box design and encryption approaches were described using an image encryption methodology and a robust S-box construction based on the Mandelbrot set.

The quality of the S-box, the primary nonlinear component in many block cipher algorithms, directly affects the encryption system’s performance and security level. As a result, the creation of the S-box with better performance has become an important field of research that many scholars are interested in. Despite the fact that many so-called strong S-Boxes have been built and passed the six criteria of bijective, nonlinearity, strict avalanche criterion (SAC), outputs bit independence criterion (BIC), differential approximation probability (DP), and linear approximation probability (LP)57, additional detection revealed that the majority of them contain fixed points or reverse fixed points, which could be a vulnerability in cryptography. The schemes58–62 contain short cycles, fixed, and reverse fixed points, and its S-boxes of nonlinearity fall between 105 and 11263.

Some researchers have constructed S-Boxes with high nonlinearity using chaotic map or algebraic operation. Al-Dweik et al.64 designed an algorithm to construct a keyed S-Box with high nonlinearity based on identical algebraic. Wang et al.65 constructed S-Boxes with high nonlinearity using a genetic algorithm, and designed a method to improve nonlinearity combining chaos with optimization operations66. Liu et al.67 proposed an efficient high nonlinearity S-Box generating algorithm based on third-order nonlinear digital filter. These constructed S-Boxes have higher nonlinearity, but most of them have weaknesses, i.e., fixed point(s), reverse fixed point(s), and short cycles. Hence, Si et al.68 constructed a 2D enhanced quadratic map, and applied it to design strong S-Boxes that satisfied two conditions: without fixed point or reverse fixed point, and without short cycle. In addition, many researchers introduced a new technique to eliminates the fixed point or reverse fixed point, and without short cycle such as69–76.

In this work, we have introduced an effective method for producing a large number of NL-S boxes based on robust cascaded chaotic maps which controlled all elliptic curve parameters.

The following points summarize the key contributions of this work:

A robust cascaded chaotic system of the two-dimensional chaotic map with large Lyapunov exponent values and robust bifurcation diagrams is introduced.The Deep convolutional neural networks are used to generate image futures as a digital signature for the used image.A new strategy for generation of secure multi-parameter multi-prime elliptic curves based on the proposed robust cascaded chaotic system is suggested.A new deep convolutional neural network chaotically controlled multipoint of multi-parameter elliptic curves over a multi-prime finite field for the large number of NL-S Boxes generation is proposed.The EC-Diffie-Hellman key exchange protocol is presented to update the NL-S Box and permutation tables, which in turn update the control and initial parameters of the proposed cascaded chaotic map.The final NL-S Box is selected based on the proposed new chaotic fuzzy selection rule system, which is selected which controlled by the proposed cascaded chaotic map.

The structure of this work is described as follows: In “2D sine cosine cross chaotic map”, the proposed cascaded chaotic map will be discussed in detail. Also, the Deep-CNN-Key Generator structure for the image future is given in “Deep-CNN-key generator”. In addition, the relation between the proposed cascaded chaotic map and the proposed MP-MP-Elliptic Curves is discussed in “Proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC”. In “Proposed robust dynamic NL-S box design”, the suggested robust dynamic NL-S Box design is presented. “Proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC image encryption”, suggests the DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC image encryption scheme based on the proposed robust dynamic NL-S Box design. In “S-box results and analysis” and “Image encryption results and analysis”, the results of simulations and security tests of the suggested NL-S-Box design and the proposed image encryption technique are introduced. In “Comprehensive study”, the Comprehensive study is discussed. Finally, “Conclusions” presents the conclusions.

2D sine cosine cross chaotic map

The 2D sine–cosine cross–chaotic map is a two-dimensional chaotic system created especially to improve image encryption methods by offering high-level diffusion and confusion. By using several beginning variables and nonlinear functions to create two different, complex pseudo-random sequences, it gets beyond the drawbacks of 1D chaotic maps, such as tiny key space and low security. (1) and (2).

As shown in Figs. 2 and 3, the small chaotic windows of the control parameters for the 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map so, it has a very narrow band which leads to a very small key space.

Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{S}{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map structure

To overcome the limitations of the 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map given in Eqs. (1) and (2), the principal of the Cascaded Chaotic system (CCS)58 is applied to the 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map in order to design a new cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map with robust bifurcation diagram and the very large Lyapunov exponent value. In this section, a new cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map has been proposed. Furthermore, the degree of sensitivity to beginning conditions and chaotic control parameters is assessed by analyzing the bifurcation diagrams and Lyapunov exponents in small and large chaotic control parameters to identify the optimal chaotic region of this novel map. For a variety of security applications, two pseudo-random sequences can be generated using the suggested cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map. The mathematical formulation of the suggested chaotic map is introduced. We introduce the mathematical formulation of the proposed chaotic map. Next, we investigate if it is capable of displaying chaotic behaviors.

Figure 5 demonstrates the CCS structure, with two seed maps, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} are two seed maps. Two seed maps are connected in series using CCS. For recursive iterations, the output of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} is fed into the input of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document}’s input, and the output of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} is then fed back into the input of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document}. The CCS is defined mathematically as follows, with two seed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document}; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{n+1}={F}_{1}\left({F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)\right)$$\end{document}. The two chaotic maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} can be different or identical. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} are the same chaotic map, the CCS becomes a structure that the map is cascaded with itself.

Fig. 5Cascaded Chaotic system (CCS) for the proposed 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map.

Cascaded Chaotic system (CCS) for the proposed 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map.

In the proposed design, the two maps are the same chaotic map (2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map). Connecting two 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map chaotic maps in series, the output sequences of CCS have the structure of cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map (or double series 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map), From the definition, the 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map includes all of its seed maps’ parameters. Consequently, compared to its seed maps, it has more parameters and complex behaviors.

Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{S}{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map behavior

In this section, the behavior cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map is analysed based on the bifurcation diagram and the Lyapunov exponent parameters. In the proposed design, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{2}\left({x}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} are the same chaotic map, the CCS becomes a structure that the map is cascaded with itself and the mathematical equations of the new cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map are modeled as:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Cx}_{n+1}=\mathrm{s}\mathrm{i}\mathrm{n}\left(\frac{1}{2}{\alpha\:}^{3}\mathrm{sec}{\left(\beta\:{cos}^{-1}\left({x}_{n}\right)\right)}^{3}\right),$$\end{document}4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Cy}_{n+1}=\mathrm{c}\mathrm{o}\mathrm{s}\left(\beta\:{cos}^{-1}\right(sin{\alpha\:}^{3}/2{y}_{n}^{3}\left)\right),$$\end{document} where sec(z) represents the secant of z.

LE of cascaded 2D SC3 map

The LE (Lyapunov exponent) describes the sensitivity to initial values. The mathematical LE calculation are given in58–60. In addition, the Lyapunov exponent calculation provides a numerical explanation of the variations between two adjacent dynamical system output values, it can be used to describe chaotic behaviors of a chaotic system. The mathematical LE calculation given in58–60 is use to analyze chaotic performance of the proposed Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map. Based on the initial conditions (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{0},{y}_{0}$$\end{document}) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\in \:\left[0,\:1\right]$$\end{document} and after first iteration, the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{1}-{y}_{1}\right|$$\end{document} is defined by53:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{1}-{y}_{1}\right|=\frac{\left|{F}_{2}\left({F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right)-{F}_{1}\left({F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)\right)\right|}{\left|\left({F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right)-\left({F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)\right)\right|}\bullet\:\frac{\left|\left({F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right)-\left({F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)\right)\right|}{\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|}\bullet\:\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|$$\end{document}

In the second iteration, (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{0}\:\underrightarrow{}{y}_{0}$$\end{document}), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\underrightarrow{}{F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)$$\end{document}, then,6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right.\right|\approx\:\underset{{F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\underrightarrow{}{F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)}{\mathrm{lim}}\frac{\left|{F}_{2}({F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)-{F}_{2}({F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)\right|}{\left|\left({F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right)-\left({F}_{1}\left({y}_{0}\right)\right)\right|}$$\end{document}7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|{x}_{0}\right.\right|\approx\:\underset{{x}_{0}\underrightarrow{}{y}_{0}}{\mathrm{lim}}\frac{\left|({F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)-{F}_{1}({y}_{0})\right|}{\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|}$$\end{document}

From Eqs. (6) and (7):8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{1}-{y}_{1}\right|\approx\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|{x}_{0}\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|$$\end{document}

Similarly, after the second iteration, (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{2}\:\underrightarrow{}{y}_{2}$$\end{document}), the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{2}-{y}_{2}\right|$$\end{document} is given by:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{2}-{y}_{2}\right|\approx\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{1}\right)\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|{x}_{1}\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{0}\right)\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|{x}_{0}\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|$$\end{document}

After n iterations, (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{n}\:\underrightarrow{}{y}_{n}$$\end{document}), the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{n}-{y}_{n}\right|$$\end{document} is given by53:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{n}-{y}_{n}\right|\approx\:\prod\:_{i=1}^{n-1}\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|\bullet\:\prod\:_{i=1}^{n-1}\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|\bullet\:\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|$$\end{document}

Based on Eq. (10), the average change in each iteration from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{0}-{y}_{0}\right|$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{x}_{n}-{y}_{n}\right|$$\end{document} is:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:\approx\:{\left\{\prod\:_{i=1}^{n-1}\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|\bullet\:\prod\:_{i=1}^{n-1}\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|\right\}}^{\frac{1}{n}}$$\end{document}

Finally, the LE of the proposed Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map can be calculated as:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:LE=\underset{n\underrightarrow{}{\infty\:}}{\mathrm{lim}}\frac{1}{n}\sum\:_{i=0}^{n-1}ln\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|{F}_{1}\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|+\underset{n\underrightarrow{}{\infty\:}}{\mathrm{lim}}\frac{1}{n}\sum\:_{i=0}^{n-1}ln\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|$$\end{document}

From Eq. (12), the LE of the first and the second chaotic map is:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:LE\left({F}_{1}\right)=\underset{n\underrightarrow{}{\infty\:}}{\mathrm{lim}}\frac{1}{n}\sum\:_{i=0}^{n-1}ln\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{2}}{dx}\left|\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|$$\end{document}14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:LE\left({F}_{2}\right)=\underset{n\underrightarrow{}{\infty\:}}{\mathrm{lim}}\frac{1}{n}\sum\:_{i=0}^{n-1}ln\:\left|\frac{d{F}_{1}}{dx}\left|\left({x}_{i}\right)\right.\right|$$\end{document}

Therefore, LE of the proposed Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map is a composite of its two seed maps’ LE values. Cascaded 2D map becomes chaotic when LE > 0 because the trajectories of its two output sequences drastically diverge as the number of iterations rises. Better chaotic performance is achieved when two trajectories diverge more quickly, as indicated by a larger positive LE value (Fig. 6).

The LE for the 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map values in relation to changes in the control parameters are displayed in Figs. 7 and 8. For example, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\beta\:$$\end{document} falls between [0.79, 1], the LE values are upward. Finally, the 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map has a small key space and small LE values which can be attacked. Compared the obtained results with 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document} map given in Eqs. (1) and (2), the control parameters of the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map have a wide chaotic range. Figures 6 and 7, shows the bifurcation diagram in relation to changing values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\alpha\:$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\beta\:$$\end{document}, for the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map. As shown in the cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map bifurcation diagram, the proposed Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map have a wide chaotic range. This indicates that the modified map has a big key space, and large LE values which strengthens the cipher’s defense against a thorough search assault. In addition, Figs. 8 and 9 shows the improving the LE values with a large value compared with the non-cascaded 2D Sine Cosine Cross Chaotic Map. This indicates that the modified map has a big key space, and large LE values which strengthens the cipher’s defense against a thorough search assault. As shown in Figs. 7, 8, 9 and 10, the proposed cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map has an expanded key space when varying α and β from 3 to 30 in the way that LE values are increased to 6.25 when α and β varying from 3 to 30.

Deep-CNN-key generator

In this section, Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) are used to generate a public key. The convolutional neural network ResNet-18, which had already been trained, was employed. More over a million photos from the ImageNet database were used to train this 18-layer deep network60. Images of pencils, mice, keyboards, and many other animals can be categorized into 1000 object categories by this pre-trained network. As a result, the network learned many attributes for a big collection of photos. Using transfer learning with pre-trained networks is frequently far simpler and quicker than training a convolutional network from the ground up. Figure 10 depicts the suggested network model architecture.

Fig. 10The ResNet 18 CNN architecture used in the proposed public key generation.

The ResNet 18 CNN architecture used in the proposed public key generation.

Proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC

The DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC is a multi-cubic curve over multi-parameters multi-primes field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{{p}_{i}}$$\end{document}, where the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{i}$$\end{document} and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:b}_{i}$$\end{document} are non-negatives with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} For single EC over prime field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{p}$$\end{document}, the element at i-th position in the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i}({p}_{i},{a}_{i},{b}_{i})$$\end{document} is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({x}_{i},\:{y}_{i})$$\end{document}. The Deep-CNN-CKG MP-MP-ECs are generated with different multi-prime and multiple non-negatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({p}_{i},\:{a}_{i},{b}_{i})$$\end{document}, so, we can define the multi-sets for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:y}_{i}$$\end{document} coordinates of all points as, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i}({p}_{i},{a}_{i},{b}_{i},\:{x}_{i},\:{y}_{i})$$\end{document}. In the each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i}({p}_{i},{a}_{i},{b}_{i})$$\end{document}, the summation of two points on each elliptic curve is achieved through the utilization of the chord-and-tangent rule, resulting in the determination of a third point residing on the curve. This addition operation, applied to points on an EC, forms a group where the point at infinity, denoted as ‘O,’ acts as the identity element. This particular group of points existing on the EC serves as the foundational structure for the development of EC cryptosystems. Geometrically, elucidating the point addition rule is the most effective approach. The proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC basics are as the standard elliptic curve cryptography theory as given in46,47. For a two different points P = (x1, y1) and Q = (x2, y2) on an EC. Suppose that the point R = (x3, y3) is the result of adding P and Q. The connected line via P and Q intersects the EC over the point called − R and R is the reflection of − R across the x axis. This point addition and doubling is shown in Fig. 11.

EC point addition: P + Q = R (x3, y3) with x3 = λ2 – x1 – x2, and y3 = λ (x1 – x3) – y1, λ = (y2 – y1)/ (x2 – x1) if P ≠ Q, and λ = (x1 + a)/2y1 if P = Q.EC point doubling: P + P = 2P for P = (x1, y1) and P ≠ − P. It is defined by 2P = (x3, y3), x3 = λ2 − 2 × 1 and y3 = λ (x1 – x3) – y1 where λ = (3 × 12 + a) / 2 y.EC point multiplication: The elliptic curve discrete logarithm problem (ECDLP) computational complexity is what determines the security of ECC. A discrete logarithm of Q with respect to the base point P is denoted by k, where k is an integer and for which Q = kP for two given points P and Q being on an EC. Even with given P and Q, however, computing the value of k becomes impossible when the order of the group of the points is large enough. A point P multiplied by a scalar k is equal to another point Q on the same curve by a series of operations that include adding and doubling points46.

EC point addition: P + Q = R (x3, y3) with x3 = λ2 – x1 – x2, and y3 = λ (x1 – x3) – y1, λ = (y2 – y1)/ (x2 – x1) if P ≠ Q, and λ = (x1 + a)/2y1 if P = Q.

EC point addition: P + Q = R (x3, y3) with x3 = λ2 – x1 – x2, and y3 = λ (x1 – x3) – y1, λ = (y2 – y1)/ (x2 – x1) if P ≠ Q, and λ = (x1 + a)/2y1 if P = Q.

EC point doubling: P + P = 2P for P = (x1, y1) and P ≠ − P. It is defined by 2P = (x3, y3), x3 = λ2 − 2 × 1 and y3 = λ (x1 – x3) – y1 where λ = (3 × 12 + a) / 2 y.

EC point doubling: P + P = 2P for P = (x1, y1) and P ≠ − P. It is defined by 2P = (x3, y3), x3 = λ2 − 2 × 1 and y3 = λ (x1 – x3) – y1 where λ = (3 × 12 + a) / 2 y.

EC point multiplication: The elliptic curve discrete logarithm problem (ECDLP) computational complexity is what determines the security of ECC. A discrete logarithm of Q with respect to the base point P is denoted by k, where k is an integer and for which Q = kP for two given points P and Q being on an EC. Even with given P and Q, however, computing the value of k becomes impossible when the order of the group of the points is large enough. A point P multiplied by a scalar k is equal to another point Q on the same curve by a series of operations that include adding and doubling points46.

EC point multiplication: The elliptic curve discrete logarithm problem (ECDLP) computational complexity is what determines the security of ECC. A discrete logarithm of Q with respect to the base point P is denoted by k, where k is an integer and for which Q = kP for two given points P and Q being on an EC. Even with given P and Q, however, computing the value of k becomes impossible when the order of the group of the points is large enough. A point P multiplied by a scalar k is equal to another point Q on the same curve by a series of operations that include adding and doubling points46.

(a) EC addition and (b) EC doubling.

Proposed robust dynamic NL-S box design

A proposed technique for building strong NL-S-boxes is presented. The technique introduced is based on the proposed Deep-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC s. In order to generate a bijective S-box, our scheme generates multiple points over the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-ECs by a deterministic hybrid chaotic integer and the deep CNN digital features and MP-MP EC points calculations. In addition, in order to resist the chosen-plaintext attacks, the proposed S-Boxes generator algorithm is designed to use both time-varying secret keys and image dependent perturbation values. The first step for the proposed NL-S Boxes generation is discussed.

NL-S box chaotic fuzzy selection rules

In this section, the proposed NL-S Box chaotic fuzzy selection rules will be discussed. The suggested cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map is used to produce a pseudo random integer sequence from 1 to 8 which are the number of NL-S Boxes generated based on Eq. (35). The total number of NL-S Boxes are 8 for i = 4. So, the chaotic fuzzy selected number (CFSN) of the NL-S Box can be computed based on output sequences x and y of the suggested cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map from Eqs. (3) and (4) as follows:33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Cx}_{n+1}=mod\left(\right(\mathrm{sin}\left(\frac{1}{2}{\alpha\:}^{3}{sec}{\left(\beta\:{cos}^{-1}\left({x}_{n}\right)\right)}^{3}\right))\times\:{10}^{10}),$$\end{document}34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Cy}_{n+1}=mod\left(\right(\mathrm{c}\mathrm{o}\mathrm{s}\left(\beta\:{cos}^{-1}\left(\frac{sin{\alpha\:}^{3}}{2{y}_{n}^{3}}\right)\right)\times\:{10}^{10}),$$\end{document}35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:CFSN=mod(\sum\:_{n=1}^{n=1000}{\left(\right(Cx}_{n+1}+\:{Cy}_{n+1})\times\:{10}^{10}),8).$$\end{document}

Finally, the chaotic fuzzy selected number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(\mathrm{C}FSN$$\end{document}) of the NL-S Box is from 1 to 8. After selected the final S-box, The fixed-point, reverse fixed-point and the short period ring problems must be check and eliminates these three problems.

Proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC image encryption

In order to verify the suitability of the S-box for image security uses, digital images are encrypted using the recommended S-box. Based on the proposed cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map both dynamic NL-P Table and dynamic NL-S Box, we use the dynamic NL-S Box and our suggested dynamic P Table to build an image encryption cryptosystem that is resistant to differential, statistical, and brute force attacks. Figure 13 depicts the architecture of our suggested cryptosystem.

Dynamic key mechanism

In the proposed cryptosystem, in order to resisting key leakage attacks, we used may techniques in order to satisfy a robust key generator mechanism. Firstly, a robust cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map is designed. This chaotic map introduced random numbers which can be a robust noised generator77,78. Secondly, as shown in Fig. 12, the DCNN used the original image as an input to generate the image futures. This image futures are used as input to the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map. So, each image has different image futures and then the all output of the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map is changed. This technique satisfies the plaintext-dependent key (dynamic keying). In addition, this technique can be prevented both known-plaintext attacks and key reuse attacks. Thirdly, the outputs of the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map are used as an input to generates different multiple public key elliptic curve cryptosystem. As shown in Fig. 13, four different generates different multiple public key elliptic curve cryptosystem with different prime field based on the elliptic curve generation conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:4{{a}_{i}}^{2}\:+\:27{b}_{i}\:\ne\:\:0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{i},{b}_{i}$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\in\:{p}_{i}$$\end{document}. The parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{i},{b}_{i}$$\end{document} and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:p}_{i}$$\end{document} are generated based on the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map. Also, bijective transformations for the chaotic sequence’s outputs are commination with the elliptic curve point generations. Finally, the updating of NL-S Box used the EC-Diffie-Hellman key exchange protocol which based on the discrete logarithm problem. The EC-Diffie-Hellman key exchange protocol is more secret and it used in exchanged and generates the initial values and control parameters for the proposed cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{C}}^{3}$$\end{document}map.

In this section, the security analysis of the proposed S-Boxes generators based on the DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC scheme is studied and evaluated with different S-Boxes tests.

Statistical performance analysis

In order to make sure that this S-box still has features like fixed point, reverse fixed point, or short iteration period rings70, the authors in69 recently examined the weaknesses for the nonlinearity of S-Boxes in AES and SM4.0 reaches 112. Fixed points or reverse fixed points can result in improper replacement, as is well known, and S-Box’s short period rings are unable to traverse every element by repeated iteration. The authors of71 examined two flaws in various S-Boxes and created an algorithm to create S-Boxes without fixed points or with reverse fixed points. Additionally, the short period rings in S-Boxes were found and removed by the study in68. An S-Box with a nonlinearity of 112 and no weakening above was built by Al-Dweik et al. in72.

Furthermore, the study described in69 determined the number of S-Boxes for the fixed point, reverse fixed point, and short iteration period rings. The results of the study presented in73 showed that the distribution of strong S-Boxes is sparse, with 3.1557 × 10,506 S-Boxes without fixed point, 1.1564 × 10,506 S-Boxes without fixed point or reverse fixed point, and 1.2255 × 10,504 S-Boxes without fixed point, reverse fixed point, or short period ring. As a result, it is quite challenging to immediately obtain a robust S-Box without weakness. A robust S-Box construction technique based on EQM was created by the authors in68. It merges all short period rings into a maximize ring and removes or reverses fixed points using nine-grid location swapping. In addition, many researchers introduced a new technique to eliminates the fixed point or reverse fixed point, and without short cycle such as69–76.

Three weakness S-Box analysis

A S-Box without fixed-point, reverse fixed-point or short period ring can be regarded as a strong S-Box. In this section, fixed point, reverse fixed point, and short iteration period rings well be studied in the proposed designed S-Boxes based of the references68–71. The fixed-point, reverse fixed-point problems can be satisfied based on design a robust chaotic map, updating the initial conditions updating based on the backtracking algorithm71. Base in Ref.71., the same concepts are used in order to generate a robust S-Boxes as shows in Fig. 14 but we used backtracking algorithm with the multi-fuzzy S-Boxes because the proposed design uses multi-elliptic curve prime factor, so, the backtracking algorithm is used for each elliptic curve prime factor S-Box generator.

After identifying the first fixed point or reverse fixed point for each elliptic curve prime factor S-Box generator71 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S\_Box\left({k}^{i}\right)}_{{k}^{i}=1}^{{k}^{i}=256}$$\end{document}), i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{Box\left({k}^{i}\right)}=({k}^{i}-1)$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{Box\left({k}^{i}\right)}=255-({k}^{i}-1)$$\end{document}, (where “ i” is the elliptic curve number) interrupt the loop, update the initial conditions until the S-Box’s without weakness is found. In addition, the reference69 uses backtracking algorithm based another technique to solve the fixed point or reverse fixed-point problem based on maximum distance separable (MDS), also, the short period ring can be eliminates based on head-tail technique69. In the proposed design, the backtracking algorithm with the multi-fuzzy S-Boxes is used to eliminates the fixed point and reverse fixed-point problem based on maximum distance separable (MDS), also, the short period ring can be eliminates based on head-tail technique for each elliptic curve prime factor S-Box generator69. If there are two fixed-point such as {63}, {6B}, which are expressed in hexadecimal format it can be eliminates these fixed points by changing the positions of the two values {63}, {6B}. Also, if there is two reverse fixed point, it can be solved by the same way in the two fixed-point.

Evaluation criteria

Bijectivity

If each element in a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:n\:\times\:n$$\end{document} S-box has a linear sum of its Boolean function f that equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:2}^{n-1}$$\end{document}32,33, then the Boolean function f is bijective. Now, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:wt\:\left({\sum\:}_{i=1}^{n}{a}_{i\:}\:{f}_{i}\right)={2}^{n-1}$$\end{document} can be used to compute the bijectivity characteristic, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{i\:}$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\in\:$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\mathrm{0,1}\right\}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{(a}_{1},\:{a}_{2},\:\dots\:,\:{a}_{n})\ne\:(0,\:0,\:\dots\:,0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:wt\:(.)\:$$\end{document}stands for humming weight. The bijective values of the suggested NL-S Box equal 128 to the ideal value, exhibiting the bijectivity characteristic. The suggested eight NL-S-boxes produced by the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC cryptosystem are compared and analyzed in Table 3.

Nonlinearity

Given a Boolean function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:f\left(x\right):{\mathrm{f}}_{2}^{n}\:\to\:{f}_{2}$$\end{document}, its nonlinearity can be expressed as follows:38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{N}_{f}=\:{}_{1\in\:{L}_{n}}{}^{min}{d}_{H}\:(f,l).$$\end{document}

The hamming distance between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:f\:\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\:l$$\end{document} is shown by the notation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\:{d}_{H}\:(f,l)$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{L}_{n}$$\end{document} is a function set that comprises all linear and affine functions. The Walsh spectrum defines the following nonlinearity33–35:39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{N}_{f}={2}^{-n}(1-{}_{w\in\:Gf\left({2}^{n}\right)}{}^{max}\:\left|{s}_{<f>}\left(\omega\:\right)\right|).$$\end{document}

Table 3 presents a comparison and analysis of the proposed eight NL-S-boxes generated by the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC cryptosystem.

Strict avalanche criterion (SAC)

In SAC, each bit in the Boolean function’s output should have a 0.5 chance of changing if one of the input bits changed. The method described in34 is used to compute the SAC of the suggested NL-S Box. Table 3 provides a comparison and analysis of the proposed eight NL-S-boxes generated by the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC cryptosystem. The SAC average values of the suggested NL-S-Box are found to be relatively near to the optimal SAC average values of 0.5. If half of a Boolean function’s output bits change in response to a change in an input bit, the function is said to fulfil SAC. A value of 0.5 is appropriate for SAC36.

Differential probability

The Differential Probability (DP) is utilized to represent the XOR distribution of the Boolean function’s input and output. One way to compute the DP is37:40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{DP}_{f}={max}_{\varDelta\:x\ne\:0,\varDelta\:y}\left(\frac{\#\left\{x \in X\left| {} \right. f\left(x\right)\theta f\left(x\theta \varDelta\:x\right)=\varDelta\:y\right\}}{{2}^{n}}\right),$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{2}^{n}$$\end{document} is size of the set and x is the set of all potential input. It is intended for this test to have the smallest computed value feasible. With a maximum value of 0.1014, Table 3 provides a comparison and analysis of the proposed eight NL-S-boxes generated by the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC cryptosystem.

Bit independent criterion (BIC)

Let us assume that the SAC is satisfied and that the two-bit output of an S-Box for a given Boolean function, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{k}(j\ne\:k)$$\end{document},, is high nonlinear. In this instance, when one of the input bits is inversed, the correlation coefficient of each pair of output bits is about equal to 0. Hence, it is possible to evaluate the BIC of the S-Box via checking that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}\oplus{x}_{k}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(j\ne\:k)$$\end{document} of any two output bits of the S-Box satisfies the nonlinearity and SAC. In a similar vein, it is optimal for the BIC that the nonlinearity values take large values, and the SAC values are closer to the ideal value of 0.538. Table 3 provides a comparison and analysis of the proposed eight NL-S-boxes generated by the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC cryptosystem. Image encryption results and analysis

In order to verify the suitability of the NL-S-box for the image security uses, digital images are encrypted using the recommended NL-S Box number 2 as given in Table 2. The suggested strategy will be implemented in grayscale for various 256 × 256 images. A number of assaults, including known-plain-text attacks, cipher-text only attacks, statistical attacks, differential attacks, and different brute-force attacks, are used to gauge how resistant an encryption technique is. The suggested method has undergone security study, and its evaluation will be based on a discussion of the NIST randomness tests, histogram, entropy, correlation coefficient, NPCR, UACI, and MSE.

NIST randomness test

The 16 statistical tests in the NIST test series57 are used to determine whether the suggested Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map is random. These tests establish whether or not the sequence that is generated is random. The probability value (σ-value) is the fundamental dependent variable in these tests. The significance level, or the boundary between the rejection and non-rejection regions, is used to compare the σ-value. The significance level in NIST is equal to 0.01. If the σ-value is less than 0.01, the sequence should be rejected because it is not random; if it is larger than 0.01, the sequence should be accepted since it is random. Table 5 presents the results of testing a binary sequence consisting of 106 bits, which was derived from the proposed authentication expansion diffusion matrix using SP800-22.

Histogram analysis

The histogram of pixel intensities of plain or cipher images is crucial tool for security analysis79. The encrypted image’s histogram needs to be uniformly flat for good encryption. Consequently, there is no information that can be disclosed about the original image, and there is no connection between it and the encrypted version. Upon visual inspection of Fig. 15, it is evident that the encrypted image’s histogram differs greatly from the original image’s histogram in a uniform manner. This is because the suggested strategy applies confusion and dissemination. Thus, it can protect against assaults based on statistics.

Fig. 15The plain and cipher images in first and third columns, respectively and the corresponding histogram plots in second and fourth columns, respectively.

The plain and cipher images in first and third columns, respectively and the corresponding histogram plots in second and fourth columns, respectively.

Correlation coefficient

In the plain image, there is a high correlation between the neighboring pixels. Stated otherwise, there should be no diagonal, vertical, or horizontal connections between adjacent pixels. This correlation should be reduced to the lowest value feasible via a robust encryption technique80. Figure 16 shows the correlation between the plain " Cameraman " image in Fig. 15a and its cipher image in Fig. 15 (k) in three primary directions: horizontal, vertical, and diagonal. The correlation coefficient between two adjacent pixels in a picture is determined using:

41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{r}=\frac{\sum\:_{i=1}^{N}({x}_{i}-E(x\left)\right)({y}_{i}-E(y\left)\right)}{\surd\:\sum\:_{i=1}^{N}({x}_{i}-E(x\left)\right)\surd\:{\sum\:_{i=1}^{N}\left({y}_{i}-E\left(y\right)\right)}^{2}}\:.\:\:\:\:$$\end{document} where, N is the data size and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(x\right)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(x\right)$$\end{document} represents the expected value of x and y and x and y are the two vectors of the original vector data and the encrypted vector data, respectively. Table 6 displays a comparison of the calculated correlation coefficients for the associated cypher picture and the plain image. The other current schemes in81 were compared with grayscale testing images. It is evident that the correlation coefficients are close to one for the original photos and close to zero for the cypher images. This suggests that the suggested plan is quite impervious to attacks based on statistics. The ciphered images have Cr values that are almost zero. Thus, the suggested system can withstand statistical attacks and has good confusion and dispersion capabilities.
