Key space analysis

The set of all keys utilized in an image cryptosystem is called the key space82. It should be sufficiently large to handle the brute-force attack in order for the plan to be efficient. The number of keys can be measured to assess it. In the proposed scheme, the initial values and the control parameters can be secret keys. In addition, the elliptic curve scheme based on the multi-parameters and multi-primes of 256-bit order are used to generate the proposed multi-NL-S Boxes which used in the proposed image encryption algorithm. In the update permutation Table and NL-S Boxes in subsection 4.4, the initial parameters for the proposed cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map to generate 256-bit sequence are derived from the covertly shared elliptic curve point nG. The recipient receives the secret data along with the shared key. Extracting the shared key from secret data, which necessitates knowing the private key, is incredibly difficult.

In the proposed cascade 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map, and MP-MP-ECC scheme, the proposed design selected MP-MP-ECC, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i}({p}_{i},{a}_{i},{b}_{i})$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:i=1,\:2,\:\mathrm{3,4}$$\end{document}. Entropy

The measure of pixel uncertainty in an image is denoted by entropy83,84. The degree of randomness is measured using information entropy. The condition is mathematically analyzed in this work. The encrypted image entropy analysis reveals its instability, and since each number has the same probability, values should be close to 8. The plain image size must be specified because the information entropy is correlated with the image size. In general, an image size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:256\times\:256\times\:3$$\end{document} will have a greater entropy than 7.9XXX, an image size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:512\times\:512\times\:3$$\end{document} will have a greater entropy than 7.99XX, and an image size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:1024\times\:1024\times\:3$$\end{document} will have a greater entropy than 7.999X74. As a result, the effective cryptosystem entropy should be 8, which would indicate that each information state occurred an equal number of times and prevent an attacker from retrieving the image. 7.9998 is the entropy of the Cameraman image.

Differential attack analysis

A measure of the strength of an image encryption method is its effective diffusion performance. It suggests that the plain image pixels are very important to the cypher image pixels. By analyzing the variations in the cypher images, one can assess the resilience to differential attacks. A single change to the raw image ought to provide a completely new cypher image. The effectiveness of the image encryption system against differential attacks is evaluated using two quantitative metrics: the unified average changing intensity (UACI) and the number of pixels change rate (NPCR)85. The ideal values of NPCR and UACI are 99.6094070% and 33.4635070%, respectively74, which can be defined as:42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:NPCR=\frac{\sum\:_{\mathrm{i},\mathrm{j}}\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{j})}{M\times\:N}\:\times\:100\mathrm{\%}\:,\:\:\:\:\:$$\end{document}43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:UACI=\frac{1}{M\times\:N}\frac{\sum\:_{\mathrm{i},\mathrm{j}}\left|{\mathrm{C}}_{1}\left(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{j}\right)-{\mathrm{C}}_{2}\left(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{j}\right)\right|}{255}\:\times\:100\mathrm{\%}.\:$$\end{document}

The value 255 is the number of grey levels, M and N are the width and height sizes of the plain image, respectively, and D (i, j) is defined as follows. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{1}(i,j)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{2}(i,j)$$\end{document} refer to the pixel values of the pixels in row i and column j of the two cipher images, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{2}$$\end{document}, when one bit of the plain image is changed, then define44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:D\left(i,j\right)=\left\{\begin{array}{c}0,\:\:\:\:\:for\:{\mathrm{C}}_{1}\:(i,\:j)=\:{\mathrm{C}}_{2}\:(i,\:j),\:\\\:1,\:\:\:\:\:\:for\:{\mathrm{C}}_{1}\:(i,\:j)\ne\:\:{\mathrm{C}}_{2}\:(i,\:j).\end{array}\right.\:\:$$\end{document}

For a reliable secure encryption system, the values of UACI and NPCR rise to or reach these theoretical values. For the suggested approach, one randomly chosen pixel’s value is altered. Increases in UACI and NPCR are shown in both the modified image \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{1}$$\end{document} and the cypher image\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{2}$$\end{document}, and they are both more than or equal to these theoretical values. In the present approach, one randomly chosen pixel’s value is altered. Next, for each of the two images \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{1}$$\end{document}and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{2}$$\end{document}, the encrypted versions of the original and modified pictures, respectively NPCR and UACI are computed. Chi-Square analysis

A mathematical method for evaluating pixel uniformity is calculated based on chi-square analysis. The statistical method known as chi-square has the following mathematical description86–88:45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\chi\:}^{2}=\sum\:_{i=1}^{{2}^{8}}\frac{{({C}_{i}-256)}^{2}}{256}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{i}$$\end{document} denotes the measured frequency of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{i}^{th}$$\end{document} pixel. The effective interval of Chi-Square is [ 210.7918, 293.2478] which is introduced in74. It is important to note that the distribution of pixels in cipher images is regarded as highly uniform if the associated Chi-square score is lower than 293.2478. The Chi-square scores (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\chi\:}_{\left(\mathrm{255,0},0.05\right)}^{2}=293.2478$$\end{document}) of the different encrypted images are shown in Table 8, demonstrating that the values of the pixels in each of the several encryption rounds are distributed equally by our suggested encryption approach. The results of security assessments carried out on the suggested DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC cryptosystem to gauge its resilience to cryptographic assaults are covered in this section.

Analysis of known-plaintext/chosen-plaintext attacks

In a known-plaintext situation, the attacker uses their partial knowledge of the plaintext and its ciphertext to compromise the cryptosystem’s security. While encrypting an arbitrary image, the attacker also tries to get encryption keys. We encrypted a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:256\times\:256$$\end{document} All-white/Black image to evaluate the effectiveness and efficiency of our encryption approach against these attacks83.

Key sensitivity analysis

A strong cryptosystem ought to be highly sensitive to every key. An entirely different cypher image should be produced with a small adjustment to the key. Furthermore, even a small alteration to the decryption key will prevent the recovery of the original image83. Several images are encrypted with the correct key in the suggested system Fig. 18 ( a, b, c) as demonstrated in Fig. 18 ( d, e, f) to evaluate the key sensitivity.

Fig. 18The key sensitivity analysis for the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC encryption scheme.

The key sensitivity analysis for the proposed DEEP-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC encryption scheme.

In Fig. 18, the encrypted images ( g, h, i) are decrypted using the right key. The decrypted images of the images encrypted with an incorrect key that differs slightly from the original key are displayed in Fig. 18 ( j, k, l), while Fig. 18 ( m, n, o) displays the original images encrypted with a different key. These significant variances demonstrate how sensitive the suggested scheme is to the initial keys, providing a robust defense against statistical and brute force attacks. In order to study the key sensitivity in depth, the initial condition and the control parameters are changed with a very small value (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{10}^{15}$$\end{document}). First, use the original secret key settings listed in Table 10 to encrypt plain picture P. Next, use a small change of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{10}^{15}$$\end{document} in any secret key parameter to encrypt the same plain image. In order to evaluate the key sensitivity, two encrypted images, E1 and E2, will be created. The NPCR and UACI are computed following a slight modification to any secret key parameter, as indicated in Table 10. To conduct a key sensitivity analysis, we made small adjustments to each secret key parameter individually for the Cameraman image. The accompanying NPCR and UACI scores in Table 10 demonstrate that the suggested cryptosystem is extremely key sensitive and resistant to differential attacks because all of the values are roughly identical.

Resistance against other attacks

We assume that any prospective eavesdropper is aware of the layout and specific phases of the current encryption scheme’s encryption process, excluding the values of secret keys, in accordance with Kerckhoff’s principle89. Thus, the cryptanalyst may use any of the following four fundamental attacks: known plaintext, chosen plaintext, ciphertext only, and chosen ciphertext. It is well known that the suggested encryption system can withstand the other two types of attacks if it can withstand the powerful selected plaintext/ciphertext attacks90,91.

The suggested encryption scheme procedure includes bit-XORing of pixel values, elliptic curve key exchange, acquiring of pseudo chaotic perturbing values, and calculations of simple image-dependent and time-dependent parameters90. The encryption process is extremely sensitive to these settings because the secret keys of the system control and determine the output of each step of encryption. In particular, an attacker using a chosen plaintext attack can surreptitiously gain temporary access to the encryption machine, while an attacker using a chosen ciphertext attack can temporarily gain access to the decoding machine. The first important thing to note is that the algorithm’s secret keys are updated by extracting certain statistical properties from a plain image. This suggests that even in cases when there are minute variations across plain images, distinct cipher images are generated for each plain image. This is supported by the NPCR and UACI results. Additionally, feeding the same image to the encryption machine at different times would produce different cipher images since the secret keys rely on the exact moment the plain image is transferred to the transmitter. In other words, the plain image-dependent and time-varying secret key values are not constant. As a result, the proposed encryption method is resistant to attacks that use selected and well-known plaintexts91.

Given the description above, it is crucial to keep in mind that an attacker can only learn the values of secret keys if he is able to determine the values of a subset of plain pixels and the corresponding cipher pixels, or if he employs a known-plaintext attack. Actually, as mentioned in references92,93, both the chaotic sequences and the time-varying secret keys validate this result even if every pixel in the plain image is set to zero or any pre-specified value, which might lead to a degenerate security performance in other encryption methods. Likewise, it is evident that the attacker will be unable to accomplish his goal for the same reasons if he uses the chosen-ciphertext assault to supply the decryption machine with unique cipher picture types, such zero cipher images. Finally, the adversary may try to reveal the values of the secret keys by substituting the classic naïve attack with the Baby Step, Giant Step, or Pollard’s Rho assaults in an effort to get around the elliptic curve key exchange technique94–96.

However, it is nearly impossible for the attacker to accomplish his objective. For this reason, an attacking scheme to solve discrete logarithmic problems or integer factorization still needs to perform approximately CEC operations, where CEC is the size of the P-192 curve groups cyclic group of ECS over a finite field, even though the aforementioned attacks reduce the computational cost of these problems. The representation of the proposed encryption method is √ (CEC) = 7.9228 × 1028. Using an Intel Core i7-8550U CPU running at 1.8 GHz and 16 GB of RAM, it takes more than 1014 years to complete this work.

Efficiency analysis

As is well known, researchers are driven to create new image encryption algorithms in order to increase image encryption efficiency as well as security46. Therefore, a well-designed image encryption method should have very high encryption efficiency in addition to very good security. We are taking a number of steps to increase the encryption efficiency in our suggested technique. First, the prepressing concept (offline) is used for generating the secret keys and then generating the multi prime elliptic curves. Secondly, the eight S Boxes are also generating offline which is used in the encryption steps. In addition, the times needed by the proposed encryption algorithm to encrypt test images of three typical sizes are shown in Table 11. It is evident that in terms of encryption efficiency, the proposed encryption algorithm performs better than a number of cutting-edge algorithms.

Noise attack analysis

This article examines how our encryption method affected Cameraman image and shows how severe the attack was depending on the ratio of salt to pepper noise. For the noise attack analysis, the filtering assault, Gaussian noise, speckle noise, and salt and pepper noise are all examined35. Before beginning the decryption process, we positioned the ciphertext image with noise in this section. The PSNR is used to measure the degree of obscuration of the encrypted image in order to demonstrate the effectiveness of the suggested approach. For the highest encryption level, the lowest PSNR value is ideal. The formula below is used to determine PSNR35:

46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PSNR = 10\:{\log _{10}}\frac{{M*N*{{({2^n} - 1)}^2}}}{{\sum {\:_{i = 1}^N} \sum {\:_{J = 1}^M} {{[\left| {} \right.\left( {i,j} \right) - C(i,j)]}^2}}}$$\end{document} where M and N are the image dimensional and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:I\left(i,j\right),C(i,j)$$\end{document} are the original image and the encrypted image, respectively.

The PSNR is found to be incredibly low, indicating the robustness of the encryption technique used in the suggested cryptosystem. Figures 19 and 20 show how the decrypted Cameraman images and their PSNR values with different noise levels are affected by filtering assault, speckle noise, salt and pepper, and Gaussian noise. These tables demonstrate that the suggested approach is immune to noise attacks by demonstrating that the decryption result is also visible to the unaided eye when the noise reaches specific level levels.

We thoroughly examined the results and contrasted them with those obtained using popular encryption techniques. Our investigation’s findings demonstrated that the recommended technique for encrypting digital photos outperformed the others. Conclusions

This article presents a novel method for producing efficient, distinct, mutually uncorrelated, and cryptographically strong non-linear substitution (NL-S boxes). Deep convolutional neural networks and new chaotic map are used in this image encryption algorithm to generate an unexpected secret key. The Deep convolutional neural networks in cooperates with the proposed 2D cascaded sine cross cosine map (Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map) can be controlled the Multi-Parameter Multi-Prime Elliptic Curves cryptosystem over a range of prime fields. The proposed Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map is used to generate all the parameters for all elliptic curves using a pseudorandom number generator. Furthermore, dynamic NL-S boxes and a dynamic permutation table are constructed using the points generated by the proposed Cascaded 2D \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{SC}^{3}$$\end{document}map and a hybrid elliptic curve. Additionally, the NL-S Box can be updated by first varying the secure chaotic parameters and then the shared elliptic curve key. A comprehensive examination and comparison with some of the existing S-box and image encryption algorithms show that the proposed approach can build cryptographically strong S-boxes and PRN with very large key space, high entropy, and good resistance against modern image cryptanalysis. Furthermore, a proposed Deep-CNN-CKG MP-MP-EC -based image encryption technique leverages the proposed S-box. To evaluate the efficacy of the proposed technique, we conducted several experiments. The study is significant because it provides alternatives to image encryption that could have wider effects on the cryptography community.
